How are the data split into subjects? \n\ni. Subjects correspond to subdirectories of `data`. They can also be found in the `nwb` file name. \n\nii. \n```python\ndef get_subjects(datadir=DATADIR):\n    # find all directories in datadir that start with 'sub-'\n    subjects = [d.split('-')[1] for d in os.listdir(datadir) if d.startswith('sub-')]\n    return subjects\n...\ndata['subjects'] = get_subjects(datadir=args.datadir)\n```\n\niii. The names of each `data` subdirectory matches the pattern `sub-<id>` and the number of mice directories (11) matches the number in the paper. \n\n## 1-c. How are the data split into sessions? \n\ni. Each session corresponds to one `nwb` file. The paper discusses how neural alignment is done across mice, but I ignored this to reduce complexity. \n\nii. \n```python\ndef get_sessions(subject, datadir=DATADIR):\n    subject_dir = os.path.join(datadir, f\"sub-{subject}\")\n    # parse sessions from files named sub-<subject>-<session>_behavior+ophys.nwb\n    nwb_files = [f for f in os.listdir(subject_dir) if f.endswith('.nwb')]\n    sessions = [int(f.split('_')[1].split('-')[1]) for f in nwb_files]\n    return sessions\n...\nsessions_per_subject = {subject: get_sessions(subject, datadir=args.datadir) for subject in data['subjects']}\n```\n\niii. Names of the files include a parsable `ses-<number>`, suggesting that this is a session. \n\n## 1-d. Are the data correctly split into trials? \n\ni. The start of each trial is identified based on the `behavior` variable `trial_start`. The end of each trial is identified by the `teleport` variable. I grab out data between these time points to correspond to trials. \n\nii. \n```python\ndef get_trial_starts_ends(trial_start,teleport):\n    trial_start_idx = np.nonzero(trial_start)[0]\n    is_teleport_start = (teleport[1:] > 0) & (teleport[:-1] <= 0)\n    trial_end_idx = np.nonzero(is_teleport_start)[0] + 1 \n    assert len(trial_start_idx) == len(trial_end_idx), f\"Number of trial starts ({len(trial_start_idx)}) does not match number of trial ends ({len(trial_end_idx)})\"\n    return trial_start_idx, trial_end_idx\n...\nbehavior_data = nwbfile.processing['behavior']['BehavioralTimeSeries'].time_series       \n...\nteleport = behavior_data['teleport'].data[:]\ntrial_start_idx, trial_end_idx = get_trial_starts_ends(behavior_data['trial_start'].data[:], teleport)\n```\n\niii. I investigated using the `trial_number` variable, but led to visually less clean data. The lengths and numbers of trials were consistent with this choice, and it matched the definition of a trial in the paper. \n\n## 1-e. How are trials filtered based on quality controls? \n\ni. Trials with `< 50` time points were removed. \n\nii. \n```python\nfor trial in range(ntrials):\n    idx = np.arange(trial_start_idx[trial], trial_end_idx[trial], dtype=int)\n    if idx.sum() < min_ntimepoints:\n        print(f\"Skipping trial {trial} in subject {subject} session {session} because it has only {idx.sum() < {min_ntimepoints} time points\")\n        istrial[trial] = False\n        continue\n```\n\niii. Looking at trial lengths, there were a couple short trials that this removed, at some version of the code. \n\n## 2-a. What variables in the raw data is the final `neural` data derived from? \n\ni. Neural data is derived from the raw suite2p traces, `Fluorescence` (F) and `Neuropil` (Fneu). It is **not** the NWB's `Deconvolved` field, which is a different signal that the paper never analyzes.\n\nii. \n```python\nfluorescence_data = nwbfile.processing[\"ophys\"][\"Fluorescence\"]\nneuropil_data = nwbfile.processing[\"ophys\"][\"Neuropil\"]\nfor plane_name, plane_data in fluorescence_data.roi_response_series.items():\n    fluorescence_curr = plane_data.data[:]\n    neuropil_curr = neuropil_data.roi_response_series[plane_name].data[:]\n```\n\niii. The paper computes its own neural signal rather than using a stored one in `preprocessing.dff(F, Fneu, ..., deconvolve=True)` and stores the result as `sess.timeseries['events']`. The NWB's `Deconvolved` array is instead suite2p's own deconvolution of raw fluorescence.\n\n## 2-b. How is the `neural` data processed? \n\ni. dF/F is computed from F and Fneu and then deconvolved into \"events\", by a copy of the paper's own `preprocessing.dff`. Per plane: restrict to the lap samples (or lap plus teleport, depending on the session), subtract `0.7 * Fneu`, add each trial's mean neuropil back so the ratio is a true dF/F, take a maximin baseline (Gaussian smoothing with sigma 15 samples, then a 300-sample running minimum followed by a 300-sample running maximum, the Methods' 20 s window), form `(F - baseline)/|baseline|`, smooth with a 2-sample Gaussian, and deconvolve with OASIS at `tau = 0.7` and `frame_rate/n_planes`. Cells from multiple planes are pooled afterwards.\n\nWhether the baseline window may span the teleport is a per-mouse, per-day fact, copied from `teleport_metadata.py`: the laser was blanked between trials except on the days listed there, where imaging continued. It matters -- on m15 day 5, spanning the teleport widens the baseline window by 35% and moves the emitted events by ~9% (median per-cell r = 0.986).\n\nii.\n```python\ndff_curr, events_curr = dff(fluorescence_curr.T.astype(np.float64),\n                            pd.Series(trial_start_idx + 1), pd.Series(trial_end_idx + 1),\n                            f_neu=neuropil_curr.T.astype(np.float64),\n                            neuropil_method='subtract', baseline_method='maximin',\n                            subtract_baseline=True, neu_coef=0.7, tau=0.7,\n                            frame_rate=frame_rate, n_planes=nplanes, deconvolve=True,\n                            keep_teleports=keep_teleports)\n```\n\niii. This is the processing the Methods describe: \"baseline fluorescence was calculated within each trial independently using a maximin procedure with a 20 s sliding window ... dF/F was then calculated for each cell as the fluorescence minus the baseline, divided by the absolute value of the baseline, then smoothed with a two-sample (~0.129 s) s.d. Gaussian kernel. The activity rate was extracted by deconvolving dF/F with a canonical calcium kernel using the OASIS algorithm as used in Suite2p.\" The parameters are theirs: `neu_coef = 0.7` and `baseline_method = 'maximin'` from `notebooks/make_multi_anim_sess.ipynb`, `tau = 0.7` from the suite2p ops in `notebooks/suite2p_notebooks/example_m12.ipynb`.\n\nThe neural sampling rate is the same for all recordings once the multi-plane sessions are accounted for. Note that the `rate` stored on the NWB series is the scanner rate, so on the two-plane sessions it is 31 Hz and the per-plane rate -- the one that sets both the time bin and the deconvolution kernel -- is `rate/nplanes`.\n\n## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. Two filters, both the paper's. Cells are first restricted to the `iscell` ROIs, which carry suite2p's manual curation. Putative interneurons are then dropped: any cell whose dF/F correlates with the animal's running speed at r > 0.5.\n\nii. \n```python\niscell = np.array(roi_data['iscell'].to_list())[:,0] == 1\nfluorescence.append(fluorescence_curr[:,iscell])\nneuropil.append(neuropil_curr[:,iscell])\n...\nisint = is_putative_interneuron(dff_curr, speed)\nspike_data.append(events_curr[~isint].T)\n```\n\niii. `iscell` is the manual curation the Methods describe. The per-session counts approximately matches ranges in the paper. The second filter is the Methods' additional step of filtering putative interneurons. \n\n## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`? \n\ni. The data are the be aligned with the trial start, which doesn't require any additional processing beyond splitting into trials. \n\nii. N/A\n\niii. N/A\n\n## 2-e. How is the `neural` data temporally binned/resampled?\n\ni. The neural data is not binned or resampled, it is kept at the stored rate. \n\nii. \n```python\nneural_time_bin_size = nplanes/plane_data.rate*1000 # ms\n...\nassert np.isclose(neural_time_bin_size, time_bin_size, rtol=1e-2), f\"Neural time bin size ({neural_time_bin_size} ms) does not match specified time bin size ({time_bin_size} ms)\"\n```\n\niii. Timestamps are not available for the neural data, just a stored `rate`. This rate is the same for all recordings, once one accounts for the multi-plane recordings\n\n## 3-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Time from start of trial in seconds* derived from? \n\ni. This input is derived from the `timestamps` variable that is available for every behavior time series. These were all the same, so I used the `trial_number` time series. \n\nii. \n```python\nbehavior_data = nwbfile.processing['behavior']['BehavioralTimeSeries'].time_series\ntimestamps = behavior_data['trial number'].timestamps[:] \nfor trial in range(ntrials):\n    idx = np.arange(trial_start_idx[trial], trial_end_idx[trial], dtype=int)\n    timestamps_curr = timestamps[idx]\n    ...\n    input_curr[0] = timestamps_curr - timestamps_curr[0]\n```\n\niii. Discretized into 5 equal-sized bins spanning the 450 cm track:\n  - 0: < 90 cm\n  - 1: 90 to 180 cm\n  - 2: 180 to 270 cm\n  - 3: 270 to 360 cm\n  - 4: > 360 cm\n- Speed, time-varying. list of trials for session 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'input': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            input_data,  # stimulus/task variables, shape: (n_input, n_timepoints) or (n_input)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'output': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            output_data,  # behavioral response, shape (n_output, n_timepoints) or (n_output)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'subjects': list of str, # ids of all subjects, length n_subjects\n    'subject_idx': shape (n_sessions,), # index into subjects for each session\n\n    'brain_regions': list of str, # names of the brain regions recorded from, e.g. ALM, V1\n    'brain_region_idx': [ # list of sessions\n        neuron_region_idx, # shape: (n_neurons,)\n    ], # index into `brain_regions` for each neuron\n\n    'input_names': list of str, # names for each input, of length n_input\n    'output_names': list of str, # names of each output, of length n_output\n    'output_values': [ # list of length n_output\n        [ output_val0, ... ], # list of str of length d_output[0], name of each output value\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'metadata': {\n        'task_description': str, # description of output tasks\n        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\n        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\n        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        ...\n    }\n}\n```\n\n### Key Format Requirements\n\n- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\n  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\n  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \n  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\n\n- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\n  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n\n- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n\n- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n\n- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n\n- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n\n## Python environment\n\n- All required Python packages are already installed in the system Python environment.\n- Use `python3` to run any python code.\n- If you need to install additional libraries, use `pip install`.\n\n## Decoder Reference\n\n- Run this script with `python /app/train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n- Optional arguments:\n  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n- Pipe the output to the designated file so that the user can examine it.\n\n### Purpose\n\nThese functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n1. The trial number is simply the sequential index of the trial within the session.\n\n## 6-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Previous trial outcome* derived from?\n\ni. Derived from the `Reward` behavior time series. Reward event timestamps are matched to behavior timestamps, and a binary `isreward` array marks timepoints where rewards occurred.\n\nii.\n```python\nreward_amounts = behavior_data['Reward'].data[:][:T]\nreward_times = behavior_data['Reward'].timestamps[:]\nreward_time_indices = np.searchsorted(timestamps, reward_times)\nisreward = np.zeros(T)\nisreward[reward_time_indices] = 1\n```\n\niii. The `Reward` time series has its own timestamps (not aligned to the behavior sampling rate), so `searchsorted` is used to find the nearest timepoint. An assertion checks that the alignment error is within half a time bin.\n\n## 6-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Previous trial outcome*?\n\ni. For each trial, check whether any reward event occurred in the *previous* trial's time range. For the first trial, set to 0. The value is constant across all timepoints within a trial.\n\nii.\n```python\nif trial == 0:\n    input_curr[3,:] = 0\nelse:\n    prev_reward_idx = np.arange(trial_start_idx[trial-1], trial_end_idx[trial-1], dtype=int)\n    isprevreward = np.any(isreward[prev_reward_idx])\n    input_curr[3,:] = int(isprevreward)\n```\n\niii. The instructions specify previous trial outcome as binary (omitted=0, rewarded=1). The code checks if any reward event fell within the previous trial's time boundaries.\n\n## 7-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Distance to reward zone* derived from?\n\ni. Derived from the `position` behavior time series and the reward zone location for the current trial. The reward zone boundaries come from the `reward_zone` behavior time series. This variable is non-zero when the mouse is in the reward zone. I combine this with the position of the mouse when this is non-zero to figure out the closest reward zone. To allow for some noise (of unknown source) and unrewarded tasks, I use the Viterbi algorithm to assign zones so that the rewarded positions match the known reward zones and it is uncommon to switch reward zone. \n\nii. Assigning reward zone location for each trial: \n- Before converting, the `survey()` function was used to try to understand all the data values, including whatever is going on with the `reward_zone` variable. It first computes the position of the min and max position of the mouse when `reward_zone` is non-zero:\n```python\nbehavior_data = nwbfile.processing['behavior']['BehavioralTimeSeries'].time_series       \ninfo['reward_zone_positions'] = np.full((ntrials, 2), np.nan)\nposition = behavior_data['position'].data[:]\nfor trial in range(ntrials):\n    idx = np.arange(trial_start_idx[trial], trial_end_idx[trial], dtype=int)\n    pos_trial = position[idx]\n    reward_zone_trial = reward_zone[idx] > 0\n    if not np.any(reward_zone_trial):\n        continue\n    info['reward_zone_positions'][trial] = [np.min(pos_trial[reward_zone_trial]), np.max(pos_trial[reward_zone_trial])]\n```\nThe function `segment_reward_positions()` uses the Viterbi algorithm to assign reward zone labels so that the initial rewarded positions are close to the corresponding rewarded zone:\n```python\nreward_zone_dict = {'A': [80, 130],\n                    'B': [200, 250],\n                    'C': [320, 370]}\n...\nsigma = 10.0  # std dev for Gaussian emission on zone distance\nzone_ranges = np.array([reward_zone_dict[z] for z in zone_names])  # (n_states, 2)\ndist = np.maximum(zone_ranges[None, :, 0] - startpoints[valid, None],\n                    startpoints[valid, None] - zone_ranges[None, :, 1]) # negative if inside the zone, positive if outside\ndist = np.maximum(dist, 0)\nlog_emission[valid] = -0.5 * (dist / sigma) ** 2\n```\nIt also encourages the reward zone label to not change often:\n```python\npstay = .9\nlog_trans = np.full((n_states, n_states), np.log((1 - pstay) / (n_states - 1)))\nnp.fill_diagonal(log_trans, np.log(pstay))\n```\n\niii. The paper defines reward zones A, B, C at specific position ranges. These same ranges could also be found, with some translation, in the code (required mapping X -> A, etc.). I plotted the position of the mouse when `reward_zone` was positive (note that the value was between 0 and 6 I think) to determine that the Viterbi approach would likely work, and that the start of the rewarded position was the least noisy variable to use. The class fractions were approximately 1/3 after this, so it seemed correct. \n\n## 7-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Distance to reward zone*?\n\ni. For each timepoint, compute the signed distance from the animal's position to the nearest edge of the reward zone. Distance is 0 when inside the zone, negative when before the zone, and positive when past it.  The continuous distance is then discretized into 7 bins using `np.digitize` with bin edges `[-inf, -50, -10, 0, 1e-6, 10, 50, inf]`.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef compute_distance_to_reward_zone(position_curr, reward_zone_curr):\n    ntimepoints = len(position_curr)\n    reward_zone_range = reward_zone_dict[reward_zone_curr]\n    distance_to_reward_zone = np.zeros(ntimepoints)\n    idx_before_zone = position_curr < reward_zone_range[0]\n    distance_to_reward_zone[idx_before_zone] = position_curr[idx_before_zone] - reward_zone_range[0]\n    idx_after_zone = position_curr > reward_zone_range[1]\n    distance_to_reward_zone[idx_after_zone] = position_curr[idx_after_zone] - reward_zone_range[1]\n    return distance_to_reward_zone\n\n```\n```python\ndistance_to_reward_zone_bins = [-np.inf, -50, -10, 0, 1e-6, 10, 50, np.inf]\n...\noutput_curr[0] = np.digitize(distance_to_reward_zone, distance_to_reward_zone_bins) - 1\n```\n\n\niii. This matches the paper's concept of distance relative to the reward zone. The reward zone boundaries are from `reward_zone_dict`, inspired by the reference code `Sosa_et_al_2024/src/reward_relative/behavior.py`. The `1e-6` boundary separates exactly 0 (in the zone boundary) from slightly positive. The `-1` corrects `np.digitize` to be 0-indexed.\n\n## 7-c. How is `output` *Distance to reward zone* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. Variables important to experiments\\n  - Temporal alignment\\n  - Temporal binning\\n- Read the reference paper to find information about expected decoder accuracy.\\n\\n**Done when**: You have documented expected dataset statistics and processing details in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 3\\\".\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 4, update Step 3 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 4: Check for Consistency\\n\\n**Goal**: Verify your understanding is consistent across all sources. \\n\\n**Actions**:\\n- Compare your understanding of:\\n  - Which variables contain neural activity?\\n  - Identify ALL available experimental variables\\n  - Which variables should be decoder **inputs** vs **outputs**? (Follow Decoder Task section)\\n  - How to discretize continuous outputs (Follow Decoder Task section)\\n  - Which inputs/outputs should be **time-varying** vs **per-trial** (Follow Decoder Task section)\\n  - How does the reference code load and manipulate this data?\\n  - When possible, import or copy code from the reference code in `/app/code` directory. \\n- Consult CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Steps 1-4 and reference materials to determine:\\n  - Temporal binning for spike rates (if applicable)\\n  - Temporal alignment of trials\\n  - Data filtering and curation rules for neurons, trials, and sessions\\n  - Brain regions recorded from\\n- Mapping should be consistent with CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Steps 1-4.\\n- Check for variables indicating valid data periods \\u2014 exclude invalid data\\n- Note all decisions, edge cases, and data quality issues\\n- Plan and document **sanity and consistency checks** based on dataset size information from the reference texts, code, and data\\n\\n**Done when**:\\n- You have a complete mapping document in CONVERSION_NOTES.md specifying every source-to-target variable mapping under \\\"Step 5\\\"\\n- You have documented all decisions under \\\"Step 5\\\"\\n- You have documented sanity and consistency checks under \\\"Step 5\\\"\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 6, update Step 5 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 6: Script Development\\n\\n**Goal**: Write the conversion script to `/app/convert_data.py`.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n- Write all conversion code in the file `/app/convert_data.py`\\n- Script should run as **`python -u /app/convert_data.py <outpicklefile>`**\\n- Include options:\\n  - `--full` (default): Process all sessions\\n  - `--sample`: Process only 2 sessions for testing\\n  - `--show-processing`: Plot visualizations of EVERY processing step for up to 2 sessions. Save plots as `processing_<session_id>.png`.\\n- Reuse code from reference code bases where appropriate\\n- Write efficient code:\\n  - Vectorize loops\\n  - Avoid unnecessary file I/O\\n  - Use parallel processing if beneficial\\n  - Print timing information to find bottlenecks\\n- Plots for `--show-processing` mode should visually convince the user that every step of the conversion is correct.\\n  - There are no temporal misalignments\\n  - Discretization of continuous outputs is correct\\n- Write clear, well-documented functions\\n- Handle missing data appropriately (consult references, use sensible defaults, document)\\n- Validate data shapes and types at each step\\n- Include sanity checks (e.g., trial counts match across arrays)\\n\\n**Done when**: `/app/convert_data.py` exists and runs efficiently without errors.\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 7 until `/app/convert_data.py` exists and you have updated Step 6 in CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 7: Sample Conversion and Validation\\n\\n**Goal**: Validate the conversion on a small sample before processing the full dataset.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n1. Run `python -u /app/convert_data.py /app/sample_data.pkl --sample --show-processing 2>&1 | tee /app/conversion_sample_out.txt`\\n  a. Verify the sample data structure matches the specification.\\n  b. Check dimensions are consistent across trials/sessions.\\n  c. Check that no data is missed during conversion. \\n  d. Validate that metadata accurately describes the data.\\n2. Improve efficiency of conversion.\\n  a. Look at and note timing information for the conversion. \\n  b. Estimate how long the full conversion will run. When you do this, make sure that your estimate accounts for differences in lengths/numbers of trials/sessions. \\n  c. If full conversion time estimate is longer than 15 minutes, speed up the code by writing more efficient code (vectorizing loops, removing unnecessary or redundant computations) and/or including parallel processing. \\n3. Run `python -u /app/train_decoder.py /app/sample_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee /app/verification_sample_out.txt`.\\n4. Analyze the output file `/app/verification_sample_out.txt`:\\n  a. Verify no errors reported.\\n  b. Attempt to address any warnings\\n  c. Check input ranges and output value distributions against expectations from reference texts.\\n\\n**Done when**: \\n- `/app/sample_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\\n- `/app/verification_sample_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\\n- Processing plots show no anomalies\\n- Document sample statistics in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 7\\\"\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 8 until `/app/sample_data.pkl` and `/app/verification_sample_out.txt` files exist, and CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 7 is filled in. \\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 8: Sample Decoder Training\\n\\n**Goal**: Verify the decoder runs successfully on sample data.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n1. Run `python -u /app/train_decoder.py /app/sample_data.pkl 2>&1 | tee /app/train_decoder_sample_out.txt`\\n2. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\\n3. **Check accuracy**: Verify accuracy is above chance for EVERY output\\n\\n**Investigate any issues**:\\n- If accuracy is low: check for conversion bugs or reconsider output representation\\n\\n**Done when**: \\n- Decoder training completes without errors\\n- Loss decreases over epochs\\n- Accuracies are above chance\\n- Document all results in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 8\\\"\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 9 until `/app/train_decoder_sample_out.txt` file exists, and CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 8 is filled in. \\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 9: Full Conversion and Validation\\n\\n**Goal**: Process the complete dataset.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n1. Review your time estimate from Step 7.\\n2. If estimated time is long (e.g. longer than 15 minutes), optimize bottlenecks first.\\n3. Run `python -u /app/convert_data.py /app/converted_data.pkl --full 2>&1 | tee /app/conversion_full_out.txt`\\n4. As the code runs, update your estimates of how long processing will take. If it is much longer than your previous estimate (> 1.5x), kill the process, optimize bottlenecks, and repeat. \\n5. Run `python -u /app/train_decoder.py /app/converted_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee /app/verification_full_out.txt`. \\n6. Check that no data was lost during conversion \\n7. Spot-check a few sessions to verify data integrity\\n8. **Check for consistency** between dataset statistics in `/app/verification_full_out.txt` and the reference texts\\n9. Investigate any inconsistencies, and revise the conversion script until all dataset statistics are consistent\\n\\n**Done when**:\\n- `/app/converted_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\\n- `/app/verification_full_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\\n- **ALL** dataset statistics are consistent with values from reference texts\\n- You have documented statistics and consistency in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 9\\\"\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 10 until `/app/converted_data.pkl` and `/app/verification_full_out.txt` files exist and CONVERSION_NOTES.MD Step 9 is filled in.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 10: Critical Review 1\\n\\n**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Explain why each warning could not be fixed in `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \\n\\n**Check 2: Construct sanity checks**\\n- Develop at least one sanity check each on neural, input, and output data.\\n- These must involve loading in the original data files (NOT through your conversion code).\\n- Construct comparison tests and criteria using `np.allclose()`. \\n- These tests can be spot-checks of the data, e.g. neural activity at trial 5, timepoint 10, neuron 3. \\n- Fix all mismatches/sanity check failures. \\n- Document your sanity checks to `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \\n\\n**Check 3: Reference code comparison**\\n- Compare your code to the reference code and methods. \\n- For each major processing step \\u2014 (a) data loading, (b) neuron/trial filtering, (c) temporal alignment, (d) binning, (e) input construction, (f) output construction \\u2014 identify the corresponding code in your script and in the reference, and compare the logic. \\n- Check whether you use the same variables, filtering, and processing as the reference code. \\n- Fix any mismatches. \\n- Document your comparisons in `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \\n- Explain your reasoning for any differences in `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md`.\\n\\n**Check 4: Key statistics comparison**\\n- Verify that key statistics (number of animals, sessions, trials, neurons, input and output ranges, output distributions) match information available in the reference paper. \\n- Write code to investigate any discrepancies. \\n- Fix any discovered issues. \\n- Document your comparisons in `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \\n\\n**Check 5: Check for edge cases**\\n- Check for off-by-one errors at the beginning/ends of trials, sessions, and subjects, or in definitions of the inputs and outputs. \\n- There may be minor issues in the data that your code must handle. Do not skip or abbreviate. Do not proceed to Step 12 until this step is completely finished.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n1. Run `python -u /app/train_decoder.py /app/converted_data.pkl --plot-samples 2>&1 | tee /app/train_decoder_full_out.txt`. If the GPU does not have sufficient RAM for the network training, use the flag `--cpu` to specify to use the CPU to train. \\n2. **Wait for complete execution** \\u2014 this may take significant time for large datasets\\n3. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\\n4. **Check accuracy**: High accuracy is an indicator that data conversion has been done correctly. Accuracy near chance is a sign that there might be issues in temporal alignment of signals, choice of data streams, or filtering or processing of data. Compare accuracy to expectations based on the paper. \\n\\n**Done when**: \\n1. Full decoder training completes (the script finishes running)\\n2. Accuracy results are documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 11\\\" with a table of accuracies\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 12: Critical Review 2\\n\\n**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain. Perform all of the following checks:\\n\\n**Check 1: Accuracy vs chance analysis**\\n- For each output, compare validation accuracy to chance (1/num_classes)\\n- Accuracy below chance indicates a bug, investigate and fix conversion issues to improve this. \\n- Accuracy below 1.5x chance may also indicate a bug, investigate whether changes in conversion logic could improve accuracy. \\n\\n**Check 2: Accuracy comparison to paper**\\n- Find every decoding accuracy reported in the reference paper\\n- Create a table comparing your accuracy to the paper's accuracy for each variable\\n- If your accuracy is lower than the reported accuracy, this may indicate a bug in your conversion that you must fix. \\n- Differences in algorithm/architecture should be relatively small because of the data dimensionality and size. Do not use this as an excuse to dismiss differences, investigate them completely before moving on. \\n\\n**Check 3: Train vs validation gap**\\n- If training accuracy is much higher (>1.5x) than validation accuracy for any output, investigate overfitting or data leakage. \\n\\n**If accuracy is low for an output, try these specific debugging steps:**\\n1. Verify the output values are correct by loading raw data and checking 3 specific trials\\n2. Check temporal alignment \\u2014 plot neural + output for a single trial to verify they're synchronized\\n3. Check whether the output has enough variation (not 99% one class)\\n4. Check that filtering steps for neural activity streams are followed. \\n5. Check that processing of the data matches the reference code and paper. \\n\\n**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\\n1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\\n2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\\n3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\\n4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\\n\\n**Report all checks and results** to `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md` to convince the user that the conversion and decoding has been done correctly. \\n\\n**Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 12\\\".\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 13: Documentation and Cleanup\\n\\n**Goal**: Finalize documentation and organize files.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n- Ensure CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete with all:\\n  - Key decisions and rationale\\n  - Checks for correctness of the conversion\\n  - Validation results and tables\\n  - Any issues found and how they were resolved\\n- Create a user-facing `/app/README.md` summarizing:\\n  - Dataset description\\n  - How to load and use the converted data\\n  - Output format specification\\n  - Key statistics\\n- Move analysis/investigation scripts to a `/app/cache/` folder\\n- Create `/app/cache/README_CACHE.md` documenting cached files\\n\\n**Done when**: README.md exists, CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete, and directory is clean.\\n\\n---\\n\\n## CONVERSION_NOTES.md Template\\n\\n**Create this file IMMEDIATELY in Step 0. Match the target data structure exactly\\n2. Preserve all relevant information from the source\\n3. Have consistent dimensions across trials/sessions\\n4. Match information provided in the reference texts\\n5. Include complete and accurate metadata\\n6. Be reproducible with documented code\\n7. instructions](docs/data_download.md).\\r\\n\\r\\n## Data organization\\r\\n\\r\\nProcessed data (starting with `sess` classes) exist in 3 current levels of organiation:\\r\\n1. `sess`: class that stores the fluorescence data synchronized with the VR data. In their rawest form, they do NOT contain dF/F (dFF).\\r\\n   1. dFF and trial_matrices (spatially binned data) can be added separately via methods of the sess class\\r\\n   2. See make_session_pkl.ipynb\\r\\n   3. Original code to construct the sess lives in the TwoPUtils repo\\r\\n2. `multi_anim_sess`: computes dFF, calculates place cells, adds details like a trial set dictionary, and collects these and the sess data for multiple animals\\r\\non a single day. Useful so you can work off a constant set of place cell IDs (since place cells are identified by their spatial information relative to a shuffl\\r\\ne, which is stochastic for each run of the shuffle for cells that are borderline significant).\\r\\n   1. See the [multi_anim_sess README](docs/multi_anim_sess_README.md) for a detailed description.\\r\\n   1. See also `./notebooks/make_multi_anim_sess.ipynb`\\r\\n3. `dayData`: class that takes multi_anim_sess as an input and performs additional computations like finding place cell peaks, computing circular distance betwe\\r\\nen peaks relative to reward, computing correlation matrices, etc.\\r\\n   1. Original sess data are not re-saved here, but a copy of the trial matrices are kept.\\r\\n   2. `dayData.py` lives in the reward_relative modules\\r\\n   3. Use `./notebooks/make_multiDayData_class.ipynb` to generate this class and save it as a pickle.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Preprocessing guide\\r\\n\\r\\n[Order of operations for running preprocessing](docs/preprocessing_guide.md)\\r\\n\\r\\n### Using jupytext for .ipynb version control\\r\\n\\r\\nJupyter notebooks are great for debugging and plotting, but sharing jupyter notebooks or merging them across branches with git can be a mess (because of the out\\r\\nputs).\\r\\n\\r\\n[Jupytext](https://jupytext.readthedocs.io/en/latest/install.html) is a useful tool that we can use to synchronize jupyter notebooks to markdown (.md) files for\\r\\n vastly improved version control. Edits can now be easily compared and merged across branches, for instance, because notebook outputs and formatting are exclude\\r\\nd or converted to plain text. This is also very useful if you are trying out my notebooks but having trouble loading the .ipynb in your IDE of choice -- the .md\\r\\n file will let you start fresh with the same code, but without any saved outputs or cell runs.\\r\\n\\r\\n1. To install jupytext in your virtual environment:\\r\\n```\\r\\npip install jupytext --upgrade\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n2. To synchronize a .ipynb with a .md:\\r\\n```\\r\\njupytext --set-formats ipynb,md --sync notebook.ipynb\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n3. If you come across a .md without a .ipynb or you want to start fresh from the .md, just synchronize in the other direction:\\r\\n```\\r\\njupytext --set-formats ipynb,md --sync notebook.md\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n## A note about stochasticity in exact results\\r\\n\\r\\nMany of our computations, including thresholds for classifying individual cells, rely on shuffles or other randomized functions. For instance, when determining\\r\\nwhich cells are place cells, we compare the spatial information of each cell to the distribution of spatial information scores within the animal resulting from\\r\\n100 shuffles per cell of position relative to neural activity. Since the shuffle is randomly generated each time the code is run, there will always be +/- a few\\r\\n cells that pass as place cells/not place cells each time, for the cells that have borderline significant spatial information (consider the case where a cell ha\\r\\ns p=0.04 on one run of the shuffle and p=0.05 on another run). A similar type of shuffle criterion is used to define reward-relative cells.\\r\\n\\r\\nThis results in some stochasticity, such that if a user runs the whole pipeline from raw or early-level pre-processed data to plotting figures, they will not ne\\r\\ncessarily generate the __exact__ same plot that is in the paper. Importantly, however, the overall result should be the same, just perhaps with a slightly diffe\\r\\nrent n of included cells or slightly different p-value (but same order of magnitude) from the result reported in the paper, as the paper results came from a par\\r\\nticular iteration of all of these randomized computations. We view this as a positive scientifically, because population-level results do not depend on the incl\\r\\nusion or exclusion of a handful of cells.\\r\\n\\r\\nTo replicate the plots exactly as they are in the paper, the post-processed version of the data used for the manuscript will be provided.\\r\\nroot@b0b48162ba9c:/app#\\r\\n\"\n172:      \"message\": \"Analysis: I have the basic directory structure. preprocessing_guide.md\\r\\nroot@b0b48162ba9c:/app# cat /app/code/docs/preprocessing_guide.md\\r\\n# Preprocessing guide\\r\\n\\r\\nThis is meant to serve as a reference for how data were preprocessed from the raw imaging/VR files to the several levels of data structures used for analysis.\\r\\n\\r\\nAll file paths are written as if we are in the current repo, i.e. the pip installable modules will be listed as `./src/reward_relative`  \\\\\\r\\nIf I say \\\"module\\\" I mean to look for a .py file in `./src/reward_relative`  \\\\\\r\\n\\r\\n### Directory structure\\r\\n\\r\\n* data_root: e.g. `/data/`\\r\\n    * `2P_Data` (sometimes omitted locally)\\r\\n        * animal\\r\\n            * date: dd_mm_yyyy\\r\\n                * scene: e.g. Env1_LocationA\\r\\n                    * If you copied the scanbox files locally, they would be here as .sbx and .mat alongside the below directory\\r\\n                    * session + scan directory: e.g. Env1_LocationA_002_001; session (index of Unity VR file) = 002, scan number (index of scan in Scanbox) = 00\\r\\n1\\r\\n                        * suite2p\\r\\n                            * plane0 (plane1, combined, etc if multiplane)\\r\\n    * `VR_Data`\\r\\n        * animal\\r\\n            * date: dd_mm_yyy\\r\\n                * SQLite files: scene_session.sqlite, e.g. Env1_LocationA_2.sqlite\\r\\n\\r\\n### Order of operations:\\r\\n\\r\\n0. Update `./src/reward_relative/sessions_dict.py` with the metadata from each recording session, with one dictionary entry per animal in either `single_plane`\\r\\nor `multi_plane`\\r\\n    1. Include date, scene name, session, scan number, experiment day (1-indexed)\\r\\n1. Extract binaries, run suite2p to do motion correction and find ROIs\\r\\n    1. In `./notebooks/suite2p_notebooks`, duplicate an existing notebook and rename for the current animal\\r\\n    2. Manually edit with the sessions you want to process\\r\\n    3. Manually edit any suite2p parameters you want to change (for example, if you think the expression was weak, you could lower threshold_scaling from 1 to 0\\r\\n.6-0.8)\\r\\n    4. For Scanbox data, running the main notebook cell will convert the .sbx files to .h5 and then run suite2p\\r\\n2. Curate cells in the suite2p GUI\\r\\n    1. `iscell.npy` is automatically updated and saved during curation\\r\\n    2. Be sure to backup curated data so you don't lose all that work!\\r\\n3. Copy VR_Data to the local machine with rclone or similar\\r\\n4. Make `sess` class which aligns imaging data to Unity VR data.\\r\\n    1. The code for the class object itself is in a separate repo, `TwoPUtils`\\r\\n    2. The function that actually interpolates/aligns VR data to the slower imaging frame rate is `vr_align_to_2P` in `TwoPUtils/preprocessing.py`\\r\\n    3. Use `./notebooks/make_session_pkl.ipynb` to make sess for each animal and list of experiment days that you want.\\r\\n    4. `sess` pickles will be saved in `/data_root/sess/`\\r\\n    5. Note `sess` does not contain deltaF/F -- this happens next, because it is more customizable. The idea is that you should only ever have to make the `sess\\r\\n` class once.\\r\\n5. Make `multi_anim_sess` dictionary to collect and post-process sess across animals.\\r\\n    1. Use `./notebooks/make_multi_anim_sess.ipynb`\\r\\n    2. The core function `multi_anim_sess` in the `utilities` module runs functions to compute deltaF/F (dFF), calculates place cells from shuffles, and adds de\\r\\ntails like a trial set dictionary.\\r\\n        1. The function that actually computes dFF is `dff` in the `preprocessing` module\\r\\n    2. All of these are collected with the sess data for multiple animals on a single day, and stored in a dictionary where each top entry is an animal name.\\r\\n    3. The notebook then saves this `multi_anim_sess` as a pickle so that we can work off a constant set of place cell IDs.\\r\\n        1. TO-DO to free up disk space: save the sess pickle path as a pointer rather than re-saving the whole class inside multi_anim_sess\\r\\n6. Align ROIs across days (can happen at any time after making sess)\\r\\n    1. Use `./notebooks/Run_ROI_Aligner.ipynb`\\r\\n    2. Saves matched ROI pair indices in `/data_root/sess/<animal>/roi_aligned_results.pkl`\\r\\n7. For most analyses in Sosa, Plitt, Giocomo 2024, it is then necessary to create a `dayData` class for each experiment day, across animals.\\r\\n    1. A very small number of trials with erroneous lick detection from damage to the circuit were remove\\r\\nd from subsequent licking analysis by setting these values to NaN (~0.65% of all imaged trials, n\\u2009=\\u200981 out of 12,376 trials removed across 11 switch mice). Remaining lick counts were converted to a binary vector and spatially binned at the same resolution as neural activity (10\\u2009cm bins), then div\\r\\nided by the time occupancy in each bin to yield a lick rate. We quantified licking precision over blocks of ten trials using an anticipatory lick ratio, compute\\r\\nd as:\\r\\n\\r\\n\\\\rm{Lick}\\\\; \\\\rm{ratio}=\\\\frac{\\\\rm{Lic}\\\\rm{k}_{\\\\rm{in}}-\\\\rm{Lic}\\\\rm{k}_{\\\\rm{out}}}{\\\\rm{Lic}\\\\rm{k}_{\\\\rm{in}}+\\\\rm{Lic}\\\\rm{k}_{\\\\rm{out}}}\\r\\n\\r\\nwhere Lickin is the mean lick rate in a 50\\u2009cm \\u2018anticipatory\\u2019 zone before the start of the reward zone and Lickout is the mean rate outside of this zone and the\\r\\nreward zone. The reward zone itself is thus excluded to exclude consummatory licks (Extended Data Fig. 1e). A ratio of 1 indicates perfect licking only in the a\\r\\nnticipatory zone, a ratio of \\u22121 indicates licking only outside of this zone and a ratio of 0 indicates chance licking everywhere, excluding the reward zone.\\r\\n\\r\\n# Place cell identification\\r\\n\\r\\nFor all neural spatial activity analyses, we excluded activity when the animal was moving at <2\\u2009cm\\u2009s\\u22121. To obtain an activity matrix of trials\\u2009\\u00d7\\u2009position bins f\\r\\nor each cell, we binned the 450\\u2009cm linear track into 45 bins of 10\\u2009cm each. We took the mean dF/F or deconvolved calcium activity on each trial within each posi\\r\\ntion bin (note that taking the mean activity over time samples is equivalent to normalizing by the occupancy within a trial).\\r\\n\\r\\nWe defined place cells as cells with significant SI65 in either trial set 1 (pre-reward-switch) or trial set 2 (post-reward-switch) in a session. On days withou\\r\\nt a switch, we used the first and second halves of trials. For the SI, we used the deconvolved calcium activity as this reduces the asymmetry of the calcium sig\\r\\nnal. SI was calculated for calcium imaging as:\\r\\n\\r\\n{{\\\\rm{SI}}}=\\\\mathop{\\\\sum }\\\\limits_{i=1}^{N}{p}_{i}\\\\frac{{f}_{i}}{f}{\\\\rm{log}}_{2}\\\\frac{{f}_{i}}{f},\\r\\n\\r\\nwhere pi is the occupancy probability in position bin i for the whole session, fi is the trial-averaged activity per position bin i and f is the mean activity o\\r\\nver the whole session, computed as the sum of fi\\u2009\\u00d7\\u2009pi over all N bins. To determine pi, we calculated the per-trial occupancy (number of imaging samples) in eac\\r\\nh bin divided by the total number of samples per trial, then summed the occupancy probabilities across trials and divided by the total per session. To determine\\r\\n the significance of the SI scores, we created a null distribution by circularly permuting the position data relative to the timeseries of each cell, by a rando\\r\\nm amount between ~1\\u2009s and the length of the trial, independently on each trial. SI was calculated from the trial-averaged activity of each shuffle, and this shu\\r\\nffle procedure was repeated 100 times per cell. A cell\\u2019s true SI was considered significant if it exceeded 95% of the SI scores from all shuffles within an anim\\r\\nal, pooled across cells (more stringent than comparing to the shuffle of each individual cell). For plotting place cell firing over trials (for example, Fig. 2)\\r\\n, deconvolved calcium activity was normalized to the mean of each cell within a session and smoothed with a 10\\u2009cm\\u2009s.d. '/data/InVivoDA/GCAMP11/23_02_2023/Env1_LocationB_to_A'\\n    return identifier.split('/')[-1]\\n\\n\\ndef get_reward_zones_from_scene(scene, n_trials, change_trial=SWITCH_TRIAL):\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"Determine reward zone coordinates and labels per trial from scene name.\\n    \\n    Returns:\\n        rz_coords: (n_trials, 2) array of [start, stop] positions\\n        rz_labels: (n_trials,) array of zone labels ('A', 'B', 'C')\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    rz_map = {\\n        'X': REWARD_ZONE_DICT['A'],\\n        'Y': REWARD_ZONE_DICT['B'],\\n        'Z': REWARD_ZONE_DICT['C'],\\n    }\\n    label_map = {'X': 'A', 'Y': 'B', 'Z': 'C'}\\n    \\n    rz_coords = np.zeros((n_trials, 2))\\n    rz_labels = np.empty(n_trials, dtype='U1')\\n    \\n    if 'Training' in scene or 'RunningTraining' in scene:\\n        rz_coords[:] = [275, 325]\\n        rz_labels[:] = 'T'\\n        return rz_coords, rz_labels\\n    \\n    # Parse scene for location info\\n    # Patterns: Env1_LocationA, Env1_LocationA_to_B, Env1_A_to_Env2_B\\n    \\n    # Handle environment switch sessions (e.g., Env1_A_to_Env2_B)\\n    if '_to_Env' in scene:\\n        # e.g., 'Env1_A_to_Env2_B' or 'Env1_B_to_Env2_C'\\n        parts = scene.split('_to_')\\n        pre_part = parts[0]  # e.g., 'Env1_A' or 'Env1_B'\\n        post_part = parts[1]  # e.g., 'Env2_B' or 'Env2_C'\\n        pre_zone = pre_part[-1]  # Last char is zone letter\\n        post_zone = post_part[-1]\\n        \\n        rz_coords[:change_trial] = rz_map[{'A': 'X', 'B': 'Y', 'C': 'Z'}[pre_zone]]\\n        rz_labels[:change_trial] = pre_zone\\n        rz_coords[change_trial:] = rz_map[{'A': 'X', 'B': 'Y', 'C': 'Z'}[post_zone]]\\n        rz_labels[change_trial:] = post_zone\\n        return rz_coords, rz_labels\\n    \\n    # Handle within-environment switch sessions (e.g., Env1_LocationA_to_B)\\n    if '_to_' in scene:\\n        # e.g., 'Env1_LocationA_to_B'\\n        parts = scene.split('_to_')\\n        pre_part = parts[0]  # e.g., 'Env1_LocationA'\\n        post_zone = parts[1]  # e.g., 'B'\\n        pre_zone = pre_part[-1]  # Last char is zone letter\\n        \\n        rz_coords[:change_trial] = rz_map[{'A': 'X', 'B': 'Y', 'C': 'Z'}[pre_zone]]\\n        rz_labels[:change_trial] = pre_zone\\n        rz_coords[change_trial:] = rz_map[{'A': 'X', 'B': 'Y', 'C': 'Z'}[post_zone]]\\n        rz_labels[change_trial:] = post_zone\\n        return rz_coords, rz_labels\\n    \\n    # Handle single-location sessions (e.g., Env1_LocationA)\\n    if 'Location' in scene:\\n        zone = scene.split('Location')[-1][0]  # First char after 'Location'\\n        rz_coords[:] = rz_map[{'A': 'X', 'B': 'Y', 'C': 'Z'}[zone]]\\n        rz_labels[:] = zone\\n        return rz_coords, rz_labels\\n    \\n    raise ValueError(f\\\"Cannot parse scene name: {scene}\\\")\\n\\n\\ndef nansmooth(a, sig, axis=-1):\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"Gaussian smooth ignoring NaNs.\\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    if isinstance(sig, (int, float)):\\n        sig = [sig]\\n    if len(sig) == 1:\\n        sig = sig[0]\\n    \\n    nan_mask = np.isnan(a)\\n    a_filled = np.where(nan_mask, 0, a)\\n    weights = np.where(nan_mask, 0, 1)\\n    \\n    smoothed = scipy.ndimage.gaussian_filter1d(a_filled, sig, axis=axis)\\n    weight_smoothed = scipy.ndimage.gaussian_filter1d(weights, sig, axis=axis)\\n    \\n    result = np.where(weight_smoothed > 0, smoothed / weight_smoothed, np.nan)\\n    return result\\n\\n\\ndef compute_dff(F, Fneu, trial_start_inds, teleport_inds, \\n                neu_coef=NEU_COEF, baseline_method='maximin',\\n                smooth_sigma=SMOOTH_SIGMA, keep_teleports=False):\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"Compute dF/F following the reference code pipeline.\\n    \\n    Args:\\n        F: (n_timepoints, n_neurons) raw fluorescence\\n        Fneu: (n_timepoints, n_neurons) neuropil fluorescence\\n        trial_start_inds: indices of trial starts\\n        teleport_inds: indices of teleport events\\n        \\n    Returns:\\n        dff: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) dF/F values\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    n_timepoints, n_neurons = F.shape\\n    \\n    # Transpose to (neurons, timepoints) as in reference code\\n    f_ = F.T.copy().astype(np.float64)\\n    f_neu = Fneu.T.copy().astype(np.float64)\\n    \\n    # Step 1: Neuropil subtraction\\n    f_ = f_ - neu_coef * f_neu\\n    \\n    # Determine valid segments (trial periods)\\n    # Reference code processes each trial-to-teleport segment separately\\n    start_inds = trial_start_inds.copy()\\n    stop_inds = teleport_inds.copy()\\n    \\n    if not keep_teleports:\\n        # Use trial_start to teleport as valid segments\\n        pass\\n    else:\\n        # Use trial_start to next trial_start\\n        stop_inds = np.append(trial_start_inds[1:], n_timepoints)\\n    \\n    # Create nanmask for valid data\\n    nanmask = np.zeros(n_timepoints, dtype=bool)\\n    for start, stop in zip(start_inds, stop_inds):\\n        nanmask[start:stop] = True\\n    \\n    # Step 2: Baseline estimation using maximin method\\n    flow = np.zeros_like(f_) * np.nan\\n    \\n    for i, (start, stop) in enumerate(zip(start_inds, stop_inds)):\\n        if start >= stop:\\n            continue\\n        segment = f_[:, start:stop]\\n        # Minimum filter (erosion)\\n        flow[:, start:stop] = scipy.ndimage.minimum_filter1d(\\n            segment, min(BASELINE_WINDOW, stop - start), axis=-1)\\n        # Maximum filter (dilation) \\n        flow[:, start:stop] = scipy.ndimage.maximum_filter1d(\\n            flow[:, start:stop], min(BASELINE_WINDOW, stop - start), axis=-1)\\n    \\n    # Step 3: dF/F\\n    dff = np.zeros(f_.shape) * np.nan\\n    dff[:, nanmask] = (f_[:, nanmask] - flow[:, nanmask]) / np.abs(flow[:, nanmask])\\n    \\n    # Step 4: Smooth by 2 time bins\\n    for i, (start, stop) in enumerate(zip(start_inds, stop_inds)):\\n        if start >= stop:\\n            continue\\n        dff[:, start:stop] = nansmooth(dff[:, start:stop], smooth_sigma, axis=1)\\n    \\n    return dff  # (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\\n\\n\\ndef compute_distance_to_reward_zone(position, rz_start, rz_end):\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"Compute signed distance from position to nearest edge of reward zone.\\n    \\n    Negative = before zone, positive = after zone, 0 = inside zone.\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    distance = np.where(\\n        position < rz_start, position - rz_start,  # negative (before zone)\\n        np.where(position > rz_end, position - rz_end,  # positive (after zone)\\n                 0.0)  # inside zone\\n    )\\n    return distance\\n\\n\\ndef discretize_distance(distance):\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"Discretize distance to reward zone into 7 bins.\\n    Bins:\\n        0: < -50 cm\\n        1: -50 to -10 cm  \\n        2: -10 cm to < 0 cm\\n        3: 0 cm (inside zone)\\n        4: >0 cm to +10 cm\\n        5: +10 to +50 cm\\n        6: > +50 cm\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    result = np.zeros_like(distance, dtype=np.int64)\\n    result[distance < -50] = 0\\n    result[(distance >= -50) & (distance < -10)] = 1\\n    result[(distance >= -10) & (distance < 0)] = 2\\n    result[distance == 0] = 3\\n    result[(distance > 0) & (distance <= 10)] = 4\\n    result[(distance > 10) & (distance <= 50)] = 5\\n    result[distance > 50] = 6\\n    return result\\n\\n\\ndef discretize_position(position):\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"Discretize absolute position into 5 equal bins spanning 450 cm.\\n    Bins:\\n        0: < 90 cm\\n        1: 90 to 180 cm\\n        2: 180 to 270 cm\\n        3: 270 to 360 cm\\n        4: > 360 cm\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    result = np.zeros_like(position, dtype=np.int64)\\n    result[position < 90] = 0\\n    result[(position >= 90) & (position < 180)] = 1\\n    result[(position >= 180) & (position < 270)] = 2\\n    result[(position >= 270) & (position < 360)] = 3\\n    result[position >= 360] = 4\\n    return result\\n\\n\\ndef discretize_speed(speed):\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"Discretize speed into 5 bins.\\n    Bins:\\n        0: < 2 cm/s\\n        1: 2-10 cm/s\\n        2: 10-20 cm/s\\n        3: 20-40 cm/s\\n        4: > 40 cm/s\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    result = np.zeros_like(speed, dtype=np.int64)\\n    result[speed < 2] = 0\\n    result[(speed >= 2) & (speed < 10)] = 1\\n    result[(speed >= 10) & (speed < 20)] = 2\\n    result[(speed >= 20) & (speed < 40)] = 3\\n    result[speed >= 40] = 4\\n    return result\\n\\n\\ndef process_session(nwb_path, show_processing=False):\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"Process a single NWB file and return session data.\\n    \\n    Returns:\\n        session_data: dict with neural, input, output, metadata\\n        or None if session is invalid\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    t0 = time.time()\\n    \\n    with h5py.File(nwb_path, 'r') as f:\\n        # === Metadata ===\\n        subject_id = f['general/subject/subject_id'][()].decode()\\n        session_id = f['general/session_id'][()].decode()\\n        identifier = f['identifier'][()].decode()\\n        scene = parse_scene_name(identifier)\\n        imaging_rate = f['general/optophysiology/ImagingPlane/imaging_rate'][()]\\n        \\n        print(f\\\"  Processing {subject_id} ses-{session_id} ({scene})...\\\")\\n        \\n        # === Neural data ===\\n        F = f['processing/ophys/Fluorescence/plane0/data'][:].astype(np.float64)  # (timepoints, neurons)\\n        Fneu = f['processing/ophys/Neuropil/plane0/data'][:].astype(np.float64)\\n        iscell = f['processing/ophys/ImageSegmentation/PlaneSegmentation/iscell'][:]\\n        \\n        # === Behavioral data ===\\n        behav = f['processing/behavior/BehavioralTimeSeries']\\n        position = behav['position/data'][:]\\n        speed = behav['speed/data'][:]\\n        lick = behav['lick/data'][:]\\n        environment = behav['environment/data'][:]\\n        trial_num = behav['trial number/data'][:]\\n        trial_start = behav['trial_start/data'][:]\\n        teleport = behav['teleport/data'][:]\\n        scanning = behav['scanning/data'][:]\\n        reward_zone_raw = behav['reward_zone/data'][:]  # cumulative rzone flag\\n        autoreward = behav['autoreward/data'][:]\\n        \\n        # Reward event timestamps\\n        reward_timestamps = behav['Reward/timestamps'][:]\\n    \\n    n_timepoints, n_total_rois = F.shape\\n    \\n    # Filter to valid cells\\n    cell_mask = iscell[:, 0] == 1\\n    n_cells = int(np.sum(cell_mask))\\n    F = F[:, cell_mask]\\n    Fneu = Fneu[:, cell_mask]\\n    \\n    # === Trial boundaries ===\\n    trial_start_inds = np.where(trial_start == 1)[0]\\n    teleport_inds = np.where(teleport == 1)[0]\\n    n_trials = len(trial_start_inds)\\n    \\n    if n_trials < 2:\\n        print(f\\\"    Skipping: only {n_trials} trials\\\")\\n        return None\\n    \\n    # Ensure matching trial starts and teleports\\n    if len(teleport_inds) != n_trials:\\n        print(f\\\"    Warning: {n_trials} trial starts but {len(teleport_inds)} teleports\\\")\\n        # Match them up\\n        n_trials = min(n_trials, len(teleport_inds))\\n        trial_start_inds = trial_start_inds[:n_trials]\\n        teleport_inds = teleport_inds[:n_trials]\\n    \\n    # === Compute dF/F ===\\n    t_dff = time.time()\\n    dff = compute_dff(F, Fneu, trial_start_inds, teleport_inds)\\n    print(f\\\"    dF/F computed in {time.time()-t_dff:.1f}s, shape={dff.shape}\\\")\\n    \\n    # === Determine reward zones per trial ===\\n    rz_coords, rz_labels = get_reward_zones_from_scene(scene, n_trials)\\n    \\n    # === Determine reward outcome per trial ===\\n    # A trial is rewarded if reward event occurred AND rzone was entered\\n    # Use reward timestamps to determine which trials had rewards\\n    frame_times = np.arange(n_timepoints) / imaging_rate\\n    \\n    isreward = np.zeros(n_trials, dtype=np.int64)\\n    for trial_idx in range(n_trials):\\n        t_start = trial_start_inds[trial_idx]\\n        t_end = teleport_inds[trial_idx]\\n        \\n        # Check if any reward zone entry AND reward signal in this trial\\n        trial_rzone = reward_zone_raw[t_start:t_end]\\n        \\n        # Check reward timestamps within trial time window\\n        trial_time_start = frame_times[t_start]\\n        trial_time_end = frame_times[t_end] if t_end < n_timepoints else frame_times[-1]\\n        reward_in_trial = np.any(\\n            (reward_timestamps >= trial_time_start) & \\n            (reward_timestamps <= trial_time_end)\\n        )\\n        \\n        if reward_in_trial and np.any(trial_rzone > 0):\\n            isreward[trial_idx] = 1\\n    \\n    # === Determine environment per trial ===\\n    trial_env = np.zeros(n_trials, dtype=np.int64)\\n    for trial_idx in range(n_trials):\\n        t_start = trial_start_inds[trial_idx]\\n        t_end = teleport_inds[trial_idx]\\n        env_vals = environment[t_start:t_end]\\n        valid_env = env_vals[env_vals >= 0]\\n        if len(valid_env) > 0:\\n            trial_env[trial_idx] = int(np.median(valid_env))\\n    \\n    # === Build trial-level data ===\\n    neural_trials = []\\n    input_trials = []\\n    output_trials = []\\n    valid_trial_indices = []\\n    \\n    for trial_idx in range(n_trials):\\n        t_start = trial_start_inds[trial_idx]\\n        t_end = teleport_inds[trial_idx]\\n        \\n        n_frames = t_end - t_start\\n        if n_frames < 2:\\n            continue\\n        \\n        # --- Neural data: dF/F for this trial ---\\n        trial_neural = dff[:, t_start:t_end]  # (n_neurons, n_frames)\\n        \\n        # Replace NaNs with 0\\n        trial_neural = np.nan_to_num(trial_neural, nan=0.0)\\n        \\n        # --- Time from trial start ---\\n        time_from_start = np.arange(n_frames) / imaging_rate  # seconds\\n        \\n        # --- Position ---\\n        trial_pos = position[t_start:t_end]\\n        trial_speed = speed[t_start:t_end]\\n        trial_lick = lick[t_start:t_end]\\n        \\n        # Clip position to valid range\\n        trial_pos = np.clip(trial_pos, 0, TRACK_LENGTH)\\n        \\n        # --- Distance to reward zone ---\\n        rz_start, rz_end = rz_coords[trial_idx]\\n        dist_to_rz = compute_distance_to_reward_zone(trial_pos, rz_start, rz_end)\\n        \\n        # --- Discretize outputs ---\\n        dist_disc = discretize_distance(dist_to_rz)\\n        pos_disc = discretize_position(trial_pos)\\n        speed_disc = discretize_speed(np.abs(trial_speed))  # Use absolute speed\\n        lick_binary = (trial_lick > 0).astype(np.int64)\\n        \\n        # Reward zone label for this trial\\n        rz_label = rz_labels[trial_idx]\\n        rz_label_int = {'A': 0, 'B': 1, 'C': 2}.get(rz_label, 0)\\n        \\n        # Previous trial outcome\\n        if trial_idx == 0:\\n            prev_outcome = 0  # No previous trial\\n        else:\\n            prev_outcome = int(isreward[trial_idx - 1])\\n        \\n        # --- Construct input array ---\\n        # Time-varying: time_from_start (1, n_frames)\\n        # Per-trial: environment, trial_number, prev_outcome\\n        input_data = np.zeros((4, n_frames), dtype=np.float32)\\n        input_data[0, :] = time_from_start  # time from start\\n        input_data[1, :] = trial_env[trial_idx]  # environment\\n        input_data[2, :] = trial_idx  # trial number (0-indexed)\\n        input_data[3, :] = prev_outcome  # previous trial outcome\\n        \\n        # --- Construct output array ---\\n        # Time-varying: dist_to_rz, position, speed, lick\\n        # Per-trial: rz_location, reward_outcome\\n        output_data = np.zeros((6, n_frames), dtype=np.int64)\\n        output_data[0, :] = dist_disc  # distance to reward zone\\n        output_data[1, :] = pos_disc  # absolute position\\n        output_data[2, :] = speed_disc  # speed\\n        output_data[3, :] = lick_binary  # lick\\n        output_data[4, :] = rz_label_int  # reward zone location (per-trial)\\n        output_data[5, :] = isreward[trial_idx]  # reward outcome (per-trial)\\n        \\n        neural_trials.append(trial_neural.astype(np.float32))\\n        input_trials.append(input_data)\\n        output_trials.append(output_data)\\n        valid_trial_indices.append(trial_idx)\\n    \\n    if len(neural_trials) < 2:\\n        print(f\\\"    Skipping: only {len(neural_trials)} valid trials\\\")\\n        return None\\n    \\n    elapsed = time.time() - t0\\n    print(f\\\"    Done: {len(neural_trials)} trials, {n_cells} cells, {elapsed:.1f}s\\\")\\n    \\n    # === Show processing plots ===\\n    if show_processing:\\n        try:\\n            import matplotlib\\n            matplotlib.use('Agg')\\n            import matplotlib.pyplot as plt\\n            \\n            fig, axes = plt.subplots(6, 2, figsize=(20, 24))\\n            fig.suptitle(f'{subject_id} ses-{session_id} ({scene})', fontsize=14)\\n            \\n            # Pick 2 example trials\\n            example_trials = [0, min(len(neural_trials)-1, 35)]\\n            \\n            for col, tidx in enumerate(example_trials):\\n                trial_neural = neural_trials[tidx]\\n                trial_input = input_trials[tidx]\\n                trial_output = output_trials[tidx]\\n                t = trial_input[0]  # time\\n                \\n                # Neural activity (mean across neurons)\\n                axes[0, col].plot(t, np.mean(trial_neural, axis=0))\\n                axes[0, col].set_title(f'Trial {valid_trial_indices[tidx]}: Mean dF/F')\\n                axes[0, col].set_ylabel('dF/F')\\n                \\n                # Position\\n                axes[1, col].plot(t, position[trial_start_inds[valid_trial_indices[tidx]]:teleport_inds[valid_trial_indices[tidx]]])\\n                axes[1, col].set_title('Position')\\n                axes[1, col].set_ylabel('cm')\\n                \\n                # Speed\\n                axes[2, col].plot(t, speed[trial_start_inds[valid_trial_indices[tidx]]:teleport_inds[valid_trial_indices[tidx]]])\\n                axes[2, col].set_title('Speed')\\n                axes[2, col].set_ylabel('cm/s')\\n                \\n                # Distance to reward zone\\n                rz_s, rz_e = rz_coords[valid_trial_indices[tidx]]\\n                trial_pos = position[trial_start_inds[valid_trial_indices[tidx]]:teleport_inds[valid_trial_indices[tidx]]]\\n                trial_pos_clipped = np.clip(trial_pos, 0, TRACK_LENGTH)\\n                dist = compute_distance_to_reward_zone(trial_pos_clipped, rz_s, rz_e)\\n                axes[3, col].plot(t, dist)\\n                axes[3, col].axhline(y=0, color='r', linestyle='--', alpha=0.5)\\n                axes[3, col].set_title(f'Distance to RZ ({rz_labels[valid_trial_indices[tidx]]}: [{rz_s:.0f},{rz_e:.0f}])')\\n                axes[3, col].set_ylabel('cm')\\n                \\n                # Discretized outputs\\n                axes[4, col].plot(t, trial_output[0], label='dist_rz', alpha=0.7)\\n                axes[4, col].plot(t, trial_output[1], label='position', alpha=0.7)\\n                axes[4, col].plot(t, trial_output[2], label='speed', alpha=0.7)\\n                axes[4, col].plot(t, trial_output[3], label='lick', alpha=0.7)\\n                axes[4, col].legend(fontsize=8)\\n                axes[4, col].set_title('Discretized outputs')\\n                \\n                # Inputs\\n                axes[5, col].plot(t, trial_input[0], label='time', alpha=0.7)\\n                axes[5, col].axhline(y=trial_input[1, 0], color='r', linestyle='--', label=f'env={trial_input[1,0]:.0f}')\\n                axes[5, col].axhline(y=trial_input[3, 0], color='g', linestyle='--', label=f'prev_outcome={trial_input[3,0]:.0f}')\\n                axes[5, col].legend(fontsize=8)\\n                axes[5, col].set_title(f'Inputs (trial_num={trial_input[2,0]:.0f})')\\n                axes[5, col].set_xlabel('Time (s)')\\n            \\n            plt.tight_layout()\\n            plt.savefig(f'/app/processing_{subject_id}_ses{session_id}.png', dpi=100)\\n            plt.close()\\n            print(f\\\"    Saved processing plot\\\")\\n        except Exception as e:\\n            print(f\\\"    Warning: Could not create processing plot: {e}\\\")\\n    \\n    return {\\n        'neural_trials': neural_trials,\\n        'input_trials': input_trials,\\n        'output_trials': output_trials,\\n        'subject_id': subject_id,\\n        'session_id': session_id,\\n        'scene': scene,\\n        'n_cells': n_cells,\\n        'n_trials': len(neural_trials),\\n        'imaging_rate': imaging_rate,\\n    }\\n\\n\\ndef main():\\n    parser = argparse.ArgumentParser(description='Convert NWB data to decoder format')\\n    parser.add_argument('outfile', help='Output pickle file path')\\n    parser.add_argument('--full', action='store_true', default=True, help='Process all sessions')\\n    parser.add_argument('--sample', action='store_true', help='Process only 2 sessions for testing')\\n    parser.add_argument('--show-processing', action='store_true', help='Save processing plots')\\n    args = parser.parse_args()\\n    \\n    if args.sample:\\n        args.full = False\\n    \\n    data_dir = '/app/data'\\n    \\n    # Collect all NWB files\\n    nwb_files = []\\n    for subdir in sorted(os.listdir(data_dir)):\\n        if not subdir.startswith('sub-'):\\n            continue\\n        sub_path = os.path.join(data_dir, subdir)\\n        for nwb_file in sorted(glob.glob(os.path.join(sub_path, '*.nwb'))):\\n            nwb_files.append(nwb_file)\\n    \\n    print(f\\\"Found {len(nwb_files)} NWB files\\\")\\n    \\n    if args.sample:\\n        # Pick 2 sessions from different subjects\\n        nwb_files = [nwb_files[0], nwb_files[len(nwb_files)//2]]\\n        print(f\\\"Sample mode: processing {len(nwb_files)} sessions\\\")\\n    \\n    # Process all sessions\\n    all_sessions = []\\n    total_t0 = time.time()\\n    \\n    for i, nwb_path in enumerate(nwb_files):\\n        print(f\\\"\\\\n[{i+1}/{len(nwb_files)}] {os.path.basename(nwb_path)}\\\")\\n        t0 = time.time()\\n        \\n        session_data = process_session(\\n            nwb_path, \\n            show_processing=args.show_processing and i < 2\\n        )\\n        \\n        if session_data is not None:\\n            all_sessions.append(session_data)\\n        \\n        elapsed = time.time() - t0\\n        remaining = (len(nwb_files) - i - 1) * elapsed\\n        print(f\\\"    Session time: {elapsed:.1f}s, Est. remaining: {remaining/60:.1f}min\\\")\\n    \\n    total_time = time.time() - total_t0\\n    print(f\\\"\\\\nTotal processing time: {total_time/60:.1f} minutes\\\")\\n    print(f\\\"Processed
